## Page 1


RATIONAL ANALYTIC SYNTOMIC COHOMOLOGY
MAXIMILIAN HAUCK
Abstract. We define and study the rational analytic syntomification XSyn of a partially proper
rigid-analytic variety X over Qp. We establish Poincar´e duality and a theory of first Chern classes
for the resulting cohomology theory, identify vector bundles on XSyn with de Rham bundles on the
Fargues–Fontaine curve of X♢and recover several classical comparison theorems in p-adic Hodge
theory. We also develop analogues of our results and constructions over Cp.

1. Introduction
Let p be a prime number.
1.1. Motivation and background. Let X be a smooth proper variety over Qp and let Qp denote a fixed
algebraic closure of Qp. The central question of p-adic Hodge theory is the following:
What can be said about the relation between de Rham cohomology RΓdR(X)
and p-adic ´etale cohomology RΓ´et(XQp, Zp) of X?
The first concrete conjectures aimed at answering this question were formulated by Fontaine in
[Fon82] and subsequently studied by many authors, most notably Faltings [Fal89], Tsuji [Tsu99]
and Nizio l [Niz08]. Following the introduction of perfectoid spaces in [Sch12], similar questions for
rigid-analytic varieties instead of algebraic varieties have also been studied in detail, particularly by
Scholze [Sch13a], Colmez–Nizio l [CN20], [CN25] and Bosco [Bos23b]. Indeed, the focus of this paper
will be this latter case of rigid-analytic varieties.
These developments have spawned a whole zoo of new p-adic cohomology theories for algebraic
or, more generally, rigid-analytic varieties over p-adic fields. On the de Rham side, this includes the
so-called log-crystalline cohomology of Hyodo and Kato from [Hyo91], [HK94], see also [Bei13], which
is nowadays often simply called Hyodo–Kato cohomology and has been adapted to the rigid-analytic
setting by Colmez–Nizio l in [CN20], and, in the geometric setting, i.e. over the completion Cp of
Qp, the B+
dR-cohomology introduced in [BMS18, §13], see also [Guo24]. On the ´etale side, Scholze
has introduced a variant of ´etale cohomology of rigid-analytic varieties called pro´etale cohomology in
[Sch13a]. Similarly to the guiding question above, much of p-adic Hodge theory is centred around
proving comparison theorems between all these cohomology theories and trying to understand how
they are related.
The idea behind syntomic cohomology is that there should be a single cohomology theory that
mediates all of these comparison theorems. It was first introduced in the algebraic setting by Fontaine–
Messing in [FM87] and later defined for p-adic formal schemes by Bhatt–Morrow–Scholze in [BMS19,
§7.4] as well as for rigid-analytic varieties by Colmez–Nizio l, see [CN20]. It is the p-adic analogue
of Deligne cohomology over the complex numbers and intimately related to K-theory and p-adic
regulators, see e.g. [BMS19, §7.4] or [NN16], and hence also of interest beyond the scope of p-adic
comparison theorems.
The major trend in p-adic Hodge theory in recent years has been geometrisation. This is the idea
that, instead of directly studying some cohomology theory attached to a variety X, one constructs a
new space attached to X which is a geometric avatar of the cohomology theory under consideration;
for this process, Bhatt has coined the term transmutation. In this approach, one recovers the original
cohomology theory by taking cohomology of the structure sheaf on the transmuted space. In the case
of de Rham cohomology, this idea goes back to work of Simpson, see [Sim97] and [ST97], and it has
entered the sphere of p-adic Hodge theory through recent work of Drinfeld and Bhatt–Lurie on such
a “stacky approach” for prismatic and syntomic cohomology of p-adic formal schemes, see [Dri22],
[BL22a], [BL22b] and [Bha22].
This geometrisation approach is advantageous for several reasons: Conceptually, it allows one to
think about cohomology theories in a geometric way and, in particular, to reinterpret comparison
theorems as certain statements about the geometry of the “transmuted” spaces. It also provides a
theory of coefficients for a cohomology theory essentially for free: one simply replaces the structure
sheaf on the transmuted space by a vector bundle or even a perfect complex. Finally, assuming that
one has a six-functor formalism for quasicoherent sheaves on the transmuted spaces, one immediately
obtains a full six-functor formalism for the original cohomology theory.
While this picture has been almost fully developed for p-adic cohomology theories of p-adic formal
schemes, much less is known for rigid-analytic varieties. The first steps in this direction have been
taken by Rodr´ıguez Camargo, who has introduced the analytic de Rham stack in [RC24], see also the
recent update [ABLB+25], and Ansch¨utz–Le Bras–Rodr´ıguez Camargo–Scholze, who have proposed a


## Page 4


4
theory of analytic prismatisation for rigid-analytic varieties with Qp-coefficients. However, the full
picture for syntomic cohomology has been missing. In this paper, we develop the theory of analytic
syntomification for rigid-analytic varieties with Qp-coefficients, making use of the framework of Gelfand
stacks recently introduced in [ABLB+25]. This is a variant of Clausen–Scholze’s analytic stacks tailored
to p-adic analytic geometry and admits a full six-functor formalism for quasicoherent sheaves.
More precisely, for any partially proper rigid-analytic variety X over Qp, we define its analytic
syntomification XSyn, which is a Gelfand stack over Qp, and taking quasicoherent cohomology on
XSyn yields a notion of rational analytic syntomic cohomology of X. For this cohomology theory, we
prove Poincar´e duality as well as the classical comparison theorems with Hyodo–Kato cohomology
and pro´etale cohomology by studying the geometry of the stack XSyn. Using similar methods, we also
recover the de Rham comparison theorem of Scholze from [Sch13a]. Moreover, if X is smooth, we
identify the category of vector bundles on XSyn with a certain full subcategory of “de Rham” vector
bundles on the relative Fargues–Fontaine curve of the diamond of X. Finally, we also provide variants
of these results and constructions for rigid-analytic varieties over Cp.
1.2. Rational analytic syntomification over Qp. We now briefly describe the construction of XSyn,
which works in the generality of X being any Gelfand stack over Qp. Namely, similarly to the algebraic
story developed by Drinfeld and Bhatt–Lurie in [Dri22] and [Bha22], we will construct an analytic
Nygaardification XN of X which receives two maps
jdR, jHT : X∆→XN
from the analytic prismatisation X∆of X, the theory of which has been developed by Ansch¨utz–Le
Bras–Rodr´ıguez Camargo–Scholze. Then XSyn is obtained by gluing the two copies of X∆embedded
into XN via jdR and jHT.
Crucial for our constructions will be the notion of nilperfectoid rings from [ABLB+25, Def. 4.6.1].
These are Gelfand rings A whose †-reduction A := A†-red is perfectoid, and they form a basis of the
!-topology on Gelfand rings. In particular, we may define Gelfand stacks by only describing their
A-valued points for nilperfectoid rings A.
With this in mind, let us first recall the definition of the analytic prismatisation Q∆

may just as well take cohomology of any other vector bundle or even perfect complex on XSyn, and
this yields a theory of analytic syntomic cohomology with coefficients. In keeping with the algebraic
case from [Bha22], the category D(XSyn) of such coefficients will be called the category of analytic
(prismatic) F-gauges on X.
Remark 1.1. We briefly sketch how to extend the definition of XSyn to integral coefficients.
In
fact, the most difficult part of this is to establish a theory of analytic prismatisation with integral
coefficients, which is the subject of ongoing joint work of the author with Ansch¨utz–Le Bras–Rodr´ıguez
Camargo–Scholze. Once this is in place, the definitions of XN and XSyn carry over verbatim. Indeed,
in our setting where X is a Gelfand stack over Qp, it turns out that the added complexity of XSyn in
the integral coefficient case then purely comes from X∆. This allows one to extend many of our results
to the case of integral coefficients.
△
Remark 1.2. The fact that our construction of XSyn takes as an input a Gelfand stack X over Qp
explains our restriction to rigid-analytic spaces which are partially proper: As explained in [ABLB+25],
partially proper rigid spaces are the ones for which the functor to Gelfand stacks is fully faithful. In
general, passing to Gelfand stacks identifies a rigid-analytic space with its Huber compactification;
e.g., the rigid disk Spa(Qp⟨T⟩, Zp⟨T⟩) is identified with Spa(Qp⟨T⟩, Zp), which has an additional
rank-2-point.
△
1.3. Main results. We now state the main results of the paper, which roughly fall into three categories:
(1) Interaction of the syntomification with six functors, (2) comparison theorems for p-adic cohomology
theories and (3) variants in the geometric setting, i.e. over Cp.
1.3.1. Poincar´e duality and Chern classes. On the foundational side, we establish basic properties of
the functor X 7→XSyn with respect to the six-functor formalism for quasi-coherent sheaves on Gelfand
stacks. The first of these concerns the case X = GSpec Qp and yields a form of Poincar´e duality for
cohomology on the stack QSyn
p

Zp in [Bha22, Cor. 6.4.6] or with local Tate duality for Galois representations. The extra shift here is
due to the fact that QSyn
p
has an extra “topological dimension” that roughly arises from the Betti stack
of an interval. However, this “extra interval” does not affect cohomology of perfect complexes on QSyn
p
by contractibility.
△

1.3.2. Comparison theorems. By studying the geometry of the syntomification, we can recover many of
the classical comparison theorems in the p-adic Hodge theory of rigid-analytic varieties and, in many
cases, upgrade them to coefficients in arbitrary perfect complexes on XSyn. This will culminate in a
description of the category Vect(XSyn) for a smooth partially proper rigid space X over Qp in terms of
de Rham bundles on the relative Fargues–Fontaine curve of X♢. In particular, this shows that vector
bundle analytic F-gauges are actually very classical objects in p-adic Hodge theory.
We begin with Scholze’s de Rham comparison theorem from [Sch13a]. For this, we first use the
geometry of XSyn to construct a functor

for any smooth partially proper rigid space X over Qp. Here, XdR,+ denotes the filtered analytic de
Rham stack of X from [RC24, Def. 5.2.2], perfect complexes on which identify with filtered perfect
complexes with connection on X.
Theorem 1.8 (Theorem 7.1). The functor
{filtered vector bundles with connection on X} →{B+
dR-local systems on Xpro´et}

from [Sch13a, §7].
Using this, the version of the de Rham comparison theorem we recover takes the following form:

pro´etale cohomology, respectively.

Theorem 1.13 (Theorem 9.4). Let X be a smooth partially proper rigid space over Qp. If E ∈Vect(XSyn)
is a vector bundle analytic F-gauge with Hodge–Tate weights all at most −i for some i ≥0, then the

Remark 1.14. Once a robust theory of analytic syntomification with Zp-coefficients is set up, we expect
that the statements as well as the proofs of Theorem 1.12 and Theorem 1.13 carry over almost verbatim
to the integral coefficient case. In contrast, this should not be expected for Theorem 1.10 as this is an
inherently rational statement.
△
Finally, we can use the results above to explicitly describe the category of vector bundles on XSyn
for smooth partially proper rigid spaces X over Qp. In particular, this allows us to relate the analytic
F-gauge coefficients that are allowed in the comparison theorems above to more classical objects in
p-adic Hodge theory. We start with the case X = GSpec Qp.

Finally, we are able to extend the above result to arbitrary smooth partially proper rigid spaces X
over Qp. In particular, this relates the category of vector bundle analytic F-gauges to the category of
de Rham local systems on Xpro´et, which are a more classical candidate for a category of “universal
coefficients” for p-adic cohomology theories attached to X.

Theorem 1.16 (Theorem 9.16). Let X be a smooth partially proper rigid space over Qp. Then there is
an equivalence of categories
Vect(XSyn) ∼= VectdR(FFX♦) ,
where the right-hand side denotes the full subcategory of vector bundles on the relative Fargues–Fontaine
curve of X♢which are de Rham in the sense of Definition 9.14. In particular, there is a fully faithful
embedding
LocdR(Xpro´et, Qp) ,→Vect(XSyn)

on X.
Remark 1.17. There should also be a variant of the above result with integral coefficients. Namely, we
expect that vector bundles on the integral analytic syntomification of a partially proper rigid space X
over Qp are equivalent to lattices in de Rham local systems on Xpro´et, with the proof being mostly
analogous to the one we give for Theorem 1.16. Indeed, one should think of the passage to integral
coefficients as adding a slope zero condition on the Fargues–Fontaine curve, which explains the fact
the we only see honest local systems in the integral coefficient case.
△
1.3.3. Syntomic cohomology over Cp. Finally, we develop analogues of the above results and construc-
tions for rigid-analytic varieties over Cp. For this, the key is to replace XSyn by an appropriate base
change, which we denote by XSyn/Cp for any Gelfand stack X over Cp. As a warning, let us already
point out that the resulting syntomic cohomology over Cp we obtain is different from the one defined

1.4. Organisation of the paper. In §2, we mostly review some aspects of the theory of Gelfand stacks
and their quasicoherent sheaves. The main novelty here is a spreading out property for perfect
complexes on a certain class of Gelfand stacks which we call “nicely coverable”. This will be proved in
§2.3 and is one of the main ingredients in our derivation of comparison theorems from the geometry of
XSyn later on. We also establish a version of the Rees equivalence in the analytic setting, see §2.4.
We then move on to reviewing the theory of rational analytic prismatisation as developed by
Ansch¨utz–Le Bras–Rodr´ıguez Camargo–Scholze in §3. While everything we discuss there is due to
them, we have still chosen to be as detailed as possible since a written account of the theory was not
yet available at the time of writing.
With these preliminaries out of the way, we can define the analytic Nygaardification and the analytic
syntomification in §4. In §4.2, we study the basic geometry of the analytic Nygaardification and how
it relates to other stacks attached to p-adic cohomology theories. Finally, we establish two technical
properties of the Nygaardification: First, we prove in §4.4 that the functor X 7→XN on derived
Berkovich spaces is compatible with ´etale localisation, which will be the key to almost all of our
d´evissage arguments in the rest of the paper. Second, in §4.5, we show that, after restricting to a
certain locus, the stack QN
p is nicely coverable in the sense of §2.3. This will be crucial for us to later
be able to apply the spreading out property for perfect complexes proved earlier.
In §5, we give explicit presentations for some Nygaardifications as quotient stacks. Namely, we
start with the case X = GSpec Qp in §5.1 and then move on to the case X = Gm in §5.2. These
presentations will be key to our proofs of the cohomological smoothness and properness results for the
syntomification. Then we study a certain closed substack of XSyn that we call XHT,†,+ in more detail
in §5.3 by not only giving an explicit presentation as a quotient, but also describing its category of
perfect complexes and their cohomology in the case where X admits a Berkovich ´etale map to the
n-dimensional overconvergent unit torus T
n. This will be the crucial ingredient for both the derivation
of the comparison between syntomic and pro´etale cohomology in low degrees and the description of
vector bundles on XSyn in terms of de Rham vector bundles on FFX♦later on.
Using the results of §5, we will be able to establish our results concerning the properties of the
syntomification with respect to six functors in §6. Here we first prove cohomological smoothness of
QSyn
p
in §6.1 and then move on to the relative case XSyn →Y Syn in §6.2. Finally, §6.3 is devoted to
establishing the existence of a strong theory of first Chern classes for syntomic cohomology of derived
Berkovich spaces in the sense of [Zav26, §5].
In §7, we start proving comparison theorems. Namely, we discuss how to interpret Scholze’s results
from [Sch13a] in terms of the syntomification by first giving an alternative construction of his functor
from filtered vector bundles with connection to B+
dR-local systems in §7.1 and then reproving the de
Rham comparison theorem in §7.2.
We then move on to the comparison between syntomic cohomology and Hyodo–Kato cohomology in
§8. We first discuss how to describe the category of perfect complexes on XSyn in terms of perfect
complexes on the Hyodo–Kato stack and on the filtered de Rham stack of X in §8.1. Then we use this
to relate vector bundles on QSyn
p
to GalQp-equivariant bundles on the Fargues–Fontaine curve which
are de Rham and to recover Bloch–Kato’s H1
g via the syntomification in §8.2.
Subsequently, we discuss the comparison between syntomic and pro´etale cohomology in §9. The
method here will be closely analogous to the one from §8 and hence we content ourselves with outlining
the steps of the proof in §9.1. Instead, the focus of this section will be §9.2, where we put this together
with our results from §5.3 in order to prove the description of vector bundles on XSyn in terms of de
Rham bundles on FFX♦.
Finally, we discuss syntomic cohomology over Cp in §10, for which we will first introduce the relevant
base changes in §10.1. The main part of this section is the stacky approach to B+
dR-cohomology, which
we develop in §10.2. Afterwards, the proofs of the comparison theorems go through just as in the
arithmetic case and hence we only give outlines in §10.3.
Notations and conventions. Throughout, we use the solid analytic ring structure on Z and, in particular,
all tensor products as well as all abelian groups are implicitly understood to be solid. Moreover,

12
all functors, e.g. tensor products or pushforwards, will always implicitly be derived and hence, in
particular, ⊗always denotes a solid derived tensor product.
All Gelfand Qp-algebras will be assumed to be separable and qfd in the sense of [ABLB+25, Def.
4.2.6] and [ABLB+25, Def. 4.2.14]. In particular, all our Gelfand stacks will implicitly be qfd.
For any Gelfand Qp-algebra A and r ≥0, we write
A⟨T⟩≤r := A ⊗Qp Qp⟨T⟩≤r ,
A{T}† := A ⊗Qp Qp⟨T⟩≤0 ,
where Qp⟨T⟩≤0 is the ring of overconvergent functions on the closed disk of radius r over Qp from
[ABLB+25, Def. 2.2.3]. Moreover, we will write A := A†-red to denote †-reductions in the sense of
[ABLB+25, Def. 2.2.19].
The term “rational localisation” will always refer to a rational localisation on the Berkovich spectrum
and hence rational localisations are always overconvergent; e.g., we have
({|f| ≤1} ⊆GSpec A) := GSpec A⟨f⟩≤1
for any Gelfand ring A and any f ∈A. We warn the reader that A⟨f⟩≤1 ̸= A⟨f⟩, i.e. this usage of the
term “rational localisation” differs from the terminology commonly used in the context of adic spaces.
Throughout, there will be a number of different twists that play a role: The Tate twist is always
denoted by Zp(1) and the Breuil–Kisin twist (on Q∆
p, QN
p or QSyn
p
) is denoted by O{1}. Finally, we
have chosen the slightly nonstandard notation O⟨−1⟩for the tautological normed line bundle on the
classifying stack ∗/T of the overconvergent unit torus.

2. Preliminaries on Gelfand stacks
We start by recording some facts about Gelfand rings and Gelfand stacks over Qp. With the
exception of §2.3, most of the material presented should be considered standard and, in particular, all
the definitions are taken from [ABLB+25].
2.1. Quasicoherent sheaves and perfect complexes on Gelfand stacks. Recall the definition of quasico-
herent sheaves on Gelfand stacks.
Definition 2.1. Let X be a Gelfand stack. Its (derived) category of quasicoherent sheaves is defined as
D(X) :=
lim
GSpec A→X D(A) ,
where the transition maps are given by pullback and D(A) denotes the category of A-modules in
D□(Qp) for any Gelfand Qp-algebra A.
We also recall that the functor
GelfStk →PrL ,
X 7→D(X)
promotes to a full six functor formalism in which all morphisms between affine Gelfand stacks
f : GSpec B = Y →X = GSpec A
are !-able and weakly cohomologically proper, i.e. there is an equivalence f! ∼= f∗. Nevertheless,
throughout most of this paper, we will mostly be considering just perfect complexes instead of all
quasicoherent sheaves. For this, we recall that this is a well-behaved notion in Gelfand stacks.
Definition 2.2. Let X be a Gelfand stack. The category Perf(X) of perfect complexes on X is defined
as the full subcategory of D(X) consisting of dualisable objects.
The association X 7→Perf(X) automatically satisfies descent, but the above definition has the
downside that dualisability in D(A) is a priori hard to control for arbitrary analytic rings A. This is
not the case for Gelfand rings:
Proposition 2.3. For any Gelfand ring A, we have Perf(A) ∼= Perf(A(∗)), where the right-hand side
denotes the usual category of perfect complexes over the discrete animated ring A(∗). Moreover, the
association
A 7→Perf[a,b](A) ,
where the right-hand side denotes the full subcategory of perfect complexes over A(∗) with Tor-amplitude
in [a, b] extends uniquely to a sheaf of categories on GelfStk.
Proof. See [ABLB+25, Prop. 3.3.5] and [ABLB+25, Lem. 4.2.11].
□
In particular, we also obtain a meaningful notion of vector bundles on Gelfand stacks:
Definition 2.4. For any Gelfand stack X and integers a ≤b, we define
Perf[a,b](X) :=
lim
GSpec A→X Perf[a,b](A) ,
where the limit is taken along pullback functors. The category Vect(X) of vector bundles on X is
defined by Vect(X) := Perf[0,0](X).
2.2. Betti stacks and their perfect complexes. Recall that there is a functor
ProN(Fin) →GelfRingop ,
S 7→GSpec Csm(S, Qp)
taking any light profinite set S to the algebra of locally constant Qp-valued functions on S.


## Page 14


14
Proposition 2.5. The above functor uniquely extends to a colimit-preserving functor
(−)Betti : d
Shv(ProN(Fin)) →GelfStk
from hypersheaves on light profinite sets to Gelfand stacks. If S is a finite-dimensional metrisable
compact Hausdorff space (seen as a light condensed set), then there is an equivalence
D(X × SBetti) ∼= Shv(S, D(X))
for any Gelfand stack X, where the right-hand side denotes the category of D(X)-valued sheaves on S.
Proof. See [ABLB+25, Lem. 4.2.9] and [Sch24, Cor. II.1.2].
□
We immediately warn the reader the we will usually not write the superscript (−)Betti when it is
clear from the context; e.g., we might write expressions like X × [0, 1] for a Gelfand stack X and then
it should be clear that [0, 1] is seen as a Gelfand stack via its Betti stack. We next want to characterise
perfect complexes on Gelfand stacks of the form X × SBetti.
Lemma 2.6. Let X be any Gelfand stack and S a CW complex. Then
Perf(X × S) ∼= LocConst(S, Perf(X)) ∼= Fun(Sing(S), Perf(X)) ,
where the middle term is the full subcategory of Shv(S, D(X)) consisting of locally constant sheaves
valued in Perf(X) and Sing(S) denotes the singular complex of S, which we view as an ∞-groupoid.
Proof. The second equivalence is a consequence of the results in [Lur17, App. A]. Since
Perf(X × S) ∼=
lim
GSpec A→X Perf(GSpec A × S)
by descent and similarly for Perf(X), proving that the two outer categories above are equivalent reduces
to the affine case X = GSpec A. Moreover, the terms Perf(X × S) and Fun(Sing(S), Perf(X)) take
colimits in S to limits (in the former case this is because colimits are universal in ∞-topoi) and hence
we may reduce to the case of a finite CW complex (even to the case of S being an n-dimensional unit
ball or an n-dimensional unit sphere).
Having made these reductions, we are now going to prove the first of the equivalences in the statement,
which then implies the second by the previous paragraph. For this, recall from Proposition 2.5 above
that there is an equivalence
D(X × S) ∼= Shv(S, D(X))
and hence, by definition of perfect complexes as dualisable objects, we have to show that the dualisable
objects of Shv(S, D(X)) are exactly the locally constant sheaves with dualisable stalks. However,
noting that dualisability of F ∈Shv(S, D(X)) is local on S because it is equivalent to the canonical
map Hom(F, 1) ⊗F →Hom(F, F) being an isomorphism, we easily see that F is dualisable whenever
it is locally constant with dualisable stalks.
For the converse, assume that F is dualisable. By passing to stalks, we see that this in particular
implies that all stalks of F are dualisable, and hence we only need to show that F is locally constant.
As S is compact Hausdorff, taking global sections commutes with filtered colimits and thus the unit
1 ∈Shv(S, D(X)) is compact. Since Hom(F, −) ∼= Hom(1, (−) ⊗F∨), this implies that F is compact
as well. However, noting that D(X) = D(A) is compactly generated and hence dualisable, F being
compact implies that it is locally constant by [Efi25, Prop. 6.15], so we are done.
□
Corollary 2.7. Let X be any Gelfand stack and S a CW complex. If S is contractible, then
Perf(X × S) ∼= Perf(X)
via pullback along the projection X × S →X.
We end our discussion of Betti stacks by recording the following gluing lemma for later use.

2.3. Spreading out perfect complexes. Before we move on, we record a technical result concerning a
certain “spreading out” property of perfect complexes on Gelfand stacks. We begin by establishing
some terminology.
Definition 2.9. A !-hypercover X• →X of Gelfand stacks is called admissible if the natural map
D(X) →lim D(X•)
is an equivalence and the same holds for any base change.
Example 2.10. By definition of the !-topology, the ˇCech nerve of any surjection Y →X of Gelfand
stacks is an admissible !-hypercover.
△
Definition 2.11. A Gelfand stack X is called nicely coverable if there is an admissible !-hypercover
GSpec A• →X
of X by affine Gelfand stacks such that all A• are n-truncated animated rings for some n ≥0. Any
such !-hypercover is called a nice hypercover of X.
Verifying that certain explicit Gelfand stacks of interest are nicely coverable will be the most
technical part of our arguments. Thus, it will be useful to establish some permanence properties of
the notion of nice coverability. In the following, when we speak about “flatness”, we will always mean
flatness with respect to the solid tensor product.
Lemma 2.12. Consider a morphism X →Y of Gelfand stacks and assume that X is nicely coverable.
If Y ′ →Y is an affine morphism of bounded flat dimension, then X′ := X ×Y Y ′ is nicely coverable as
well.
Proof. If GSpec A• →X is a nice hypercover such that all A• are n-truncated and the flat dimension
of Y ′ →Y is bounded by m ≥0, then GSpec A• ×Y Y ′ →X′ will be an admissible hypercover where
all GSpec A• ×Y Y ′ are affine and (n + m)-truncated.
□
The next lemma says that being nicely coverable is basically local for admissible hypercovers up to
an additional uniformity assumption.
Lemma 2.13. Let X• →X be an admissible !-hypercover of Gelfand stacks. If there is some fixed n ≥0
such that any X• admits an admissible !-hypercover by n-truncated affine Gelfand stacks, then X is
nicely coverable.
Proof. For each j ≥0, let GSpec Aj

stacks. Considering
B• := A•

The following lemma shows that nice coverability is preserved under passing to finite ´etale covers

Lemma 2.16. Let X →Y be a morphism of Gelfand stacks which becomes finite ´etale after pulling
back to any totally disconnected nilperfectoid GSpec A →Y over Y . If Y is nicely coverable, then X is
nicely coverable as well. Moreover, the same conclusion holds if we replace “finite ´etale” by “rational
localisation”.

map colimn Qp⟨T1, . . . , Tn⟩→Qcycl

nice hypercover of Y whose zeroth term is totally disconnected nilperfectoid. In other words, we may
assume that Y0 is totally disconnected nilperfectoid and then the base change X0 := Y0 ×Y X is finite

affine and n-truncated as well; moreover, as admissible hypercovers are stable under base change, we
conclude that X• →X is a nice hypercover and this finishes the proof in the finite ´etale case.
In the case where X →Y becomes a rational localisation after base change to any strictly
totally disconnected nilperfectoid over Y , one argues similarly: As the base change X ×Y GSpec A
is defined by finitely many inequalities of the form |f| ≤1 or |f| ≥1 inside GSpec A, the map
X ×Y GSpec A →GSpec A has finite flat dimension by Lemma 2.14 and thus nice hypercovers are
preserved by base change along this map by Lemma 2.12.

Corollary 2.25. Let A be any Gelfand ring. Then there is an equivalence between perfect complexes on

conditions:

Perf(A1/Gm) ∼= Perf(D/T).

2.5. p-adic Lie groups as Gelfand stacks. Before we finally discuss de Rham and Hyodo–Kato stacks,
let us quickly recall the different ways we can view p-adic Lie groups as Gelfand stacks.
The first such way in fact works for any group object G in (light) condensed anima: it simply takes
G to the Gelfand stack GBetti, which will be a group stack by functoriality. Recall that, in the special
case where G is a profinite group, e.g. a compact p-adic Lie group, the latter is just given by
GBetti = GSpec Csm(G, Qp) ,
where Csm(G, Qp) denotes the algebra of locally constant Qp-valued functions on G. Owing to the
fact that representations of (locally) profinite groups where each orbit map factors through a finite (or
discrete) quotient are usually called smooth representations, we introduce a slightly different notation
for GBetti in this context.
Definition 2.27. Let G be a locally profinite group, e.g. a p-adic Lie group. Then we write Gsm to
denote the group stack GBetti.
In keeping with the notation, for any locally profinite group G, by [RJRC25, Prop. 5.4.2], there is
an equivalence
D(∗/Gsm) ∼= D(Repsm
Qp(G))
between the derived category of quasicoherent sheaves on the classifying stack ∗/Gsm and the derived
category of smooth G-representations on solid Qp-vector spaces in the sense of [RJRC25, §5].
What if we are instead interested in locally analytic representations? Following [RJRC25], we should
make the following definition.
Definition 2.28. Let G be a p-adic Lie group and G0 ⊆G a compact open subgroup. We define the
group stack Gla as
Gla :=
a
g∈G/G0
GSpec Cla(G0, Qp) ,
where Cla(G0, Qp) denotes the algebra of Qp-valued locally analytic functions on G0.
Let us point out that one can show that the above definition is independent of the choice of G0.
Moreover, as before in the case of smooth representations, by [RJRC25, Thm. 4.3.3], for any p-adic Lie
group G, there is an equivalence
D(∗/Gla) ∼= D(Repla
Qp(G))
between the derived categories of quasicoherent sheaves on ∗/Gla and locally analytic G-representations
on solid Qp-vector spaces in the sense of [RJRC25, §3].
Definition 2.29. Let G be a locally profinite group and G0 ⊆G a compact open subgroup. The group
stack Gcont is defined as
Gcont :=
a
g∈G/G0
GSpec Ccont(G0, Qp) ,
where Ccont(G0, Qp) denotes the algebra of Qp-valued continuous functions on G0.


## Page 24


24
Let us stress that, while one should think of quasicoherent sheaves on ∗/Gcont as continuous G-
representations on solid Qp-vector spaces for a locally profinite group G, it is not true that D(∗/Gcont)
embeds fully faithfully into the derived category D(Qp[G]) of modules over the solid group algebra
Qp[G], which is usually called the Iwasawa algebra of G.
Now letting G be a p-adic Lie group, the natural maps
Csm(G0, Qp) →Cla(G0, Qp) →Ccont(G0, Qp)
for a compact open subgroup G0 induce corresponding maps of group stacks
Gcont →Gla →Gsm .
In particular, we obtain a chain of maps
∗/Gcont
f−→∗/Gla
g−→∗/Gsm
between the corresponding classifying stacks. In terms of representations, pushforward along f then
corresponds to taking locally analytic vectors in the sense of [RJRC25, §3] while pushforward along g
corresponds to taking Lie algebra cohomology, see [RJRC25, Prop. 6.2.1], while the respective pullback
functors just correspond to forgetful functors.
2.6. De Rham stacks. Finally, we recall the definitions and some statements surrounding the analytic
de Rham stack, which was first introduced in [RC24] and has recently been revisited in [ABLB+25] in
the setting of Gelfand stacks. For this, first recall that, in loc. cit., the authors have defined a category
arcStk of (qfd) arc-stacks over Qp, which are hypersheaves on (separable qfd) perfectoid Qp-algebras
for the arc-topology introduced in [Sch26a], and we will use the notation Spd A to refer to the arc-stack
represented by a perfectoid Qp-algebra A.
For any Gelfand stack X, we can get an arc-stack denoted by X♢by restricting its functor of points
to perfectoid rings. This yields a functor
(−)♢: GelfStk →arcStk
called the diamond, which has a left-adjoint given by extending the assignment Spd A 7→GSpec A
by colimits. While we will usually not make use of this left-adjoint directly, what we will use is its
precomposition with (−)♢:
Definition 2.30. The postcomposition of (−)♢: GelfStk →arcStk with its left-adjoint is called the
perfectoidisation and denoted by
d
(−) : GelfStk →GelfStk .
Note that, by definition, any Gelfand stack X receives a natural map from its perfectoidisation “
X.
The diamond functor also has a right-adjoint.
Definition 2.31. The right-adjoint of the functor (−)♢: GelfStk →arcStk is called the analytic de
Rham stack and denoted
(−)dR : arcStk →GelfStk .
We will also use the same name and notation to refer to the precomposition of this functor with
(−)♢: GelfStk →arcStk.
Again, note that, by definition, there is always a natural map X →XdR for any Gelfand stack X.

To explicitly describe the functor of points of the de Rham stack of a given arc-stack, first recall
from [ABLB+25, Prop. 4.6.4] that any Gelfand ring admits a !-cover by a ring of the following form.
Definition 2.34. A Gelfand ring A is called nilperfectoid if its †-reduction A is perfectoid. It is moreover
called totally disconnected if the Berkovich space M(A) = M(A) is a profinite set.
Now given any arc-stack X, it suffices to specify the functor of points of XdR on nilperfectoid rings
A, and there it is given by the formula
XdR(A) = X(A) ,
see [ABLB+25, Prop. 4.6.5]. If X is a Gelfand stack, this shows that XdR is obtained by sheafifying
the assignment A 7→X(A) for Gelfand rings A.
One of the main results of [ABLB+25] is the fact that the functor
(−)dR : arcStk →GelfStk
commutes with colimits, which is also what enables Example 2.32 above. Moreover, it calculates de
Rham cohomology of partially proper rigid analytic spaces.
Proposition 2.35. For any smooth partially proper rigid space X over Qp, there is a natural isomorphism
RΓ(XdR, O) ∼= RΓdR(X) ,
where the right-hand side denotes usual de Rham cohomology of X. Moreover, there is an equivalence
Vect(XdR) ∼= {vector bundles with connection on X} ,
where the connection in the target category is always assumed to be flat.
Proof. See [ABLB+25, Prop. 5.2.1, Rem. 5.2.3].
□
The analytic de Rham stack also has a filtered variant. While this is usually defined as living over
the quotient A1/Gm, it will be more convenient for us to use D/T as the base, where we recall that
D denotes the overconvergent unit disk and T is the overconvergent unit torus. Recall that we have
already shown in Lemma 2.21 that D/T and A1/Gm have equivalent categories of perfect complexes;
more precisely, by Proposition 2.23, perfect complexes on both of these stacks are equivalent to finitely
filtered perfect complexes over Qp.
Let us also shortly comment on how the moduli interpretations of D/T and A1/Gm compare. For
this, recall that the latter parametrises generalised Cartier divisors, i.e. maps L →A, where L is an
invertible A-module (note that we implicitly need to use Proposition 2.3 here). A similar description
holds for A-points of D/T.
Definition 2.36. Let A be a Gelfand ring. A normed line bundle over A is a line bundle L over A which
is !-locally equipped with an invertible A≤1-submodule L≤1 ⊆L.


## Page 26

Remark 2.37. It is probably not true that one can always obtain an actual A≤1-module L≤1 from a
normed line bundle L over A, i.e. without having to !-localise.
△
Definition 2.38. Let A be a Gelfand ring. A normed generalised Cartier divisor of norm at most r ≥0
is a normed line bundle L over A together with a map L →A such that, !-locally on A, the composite
L≤1 →L →A factors through A≤r.
Now it is clear from the definition that the quotient D/T exactly parametrises normed generalised
Cartier divisors of norm at most 1. Moreover, given such a divisor corresponding to a map GSpec A →
D/T, postcomposing with the map D/T →A1/Gm exactly corresponds to forgetting the norm. We
can now define the filtered version of XdR.

is the Gelfand stack over D/T obtained by sheafifying the assignment

where L →A is the normed generalised Cartier divisor classified by the map GSpec A →D/T.

According to [ABLB+25, Rem. 6.1.3], it is expected that the above definition agrees with classical
definitions of Hyodo–Kato cohomology. More specifically, one should be able to deduce this from
the fact that the cohomology of the Hyodo–Kato stack defines a realisation of Berkovich motives
according to the recent PhD thesis of Ko Aoki, see [Aok26, Thm. 1.7], together with the interpretation
of Hyodo–Kato cohomology via motives from [BGV24]. While we will not use this expected relation in
any of our arguments, we will still implicitly assume it when we formulate some of our theorems in
more classical language or compare them to previous results.

3. Analytic prismatisation over Qp
We now recall some facts about (rational) analytic prismatisation of Gelfand stacks over Qp, which
has been developed by Ansch¨utz–Le Bras–Rodr´ıguez Camargo–Scholze. From the outset, let us
emphasise that, because we restrict both our geometry and coefficients to characteristic 0, i.e. we allow
Gelfand stacks over Qp as inputs and our cohomology theories will have Qp-coefficients, the overall
situation is very simple: Basically, the rational analytic prismatisation X∆of a Gelfand stack X over
Qp will just combine the Hyodo–Kato stack XHK and the stack XDiv1 from [ALBRCS], the definition
of which we will review further below.

Also note that, for us, a Cartier divisor on an arbitrary Gelfand stack is defined as a map X →A1/Gm.

Definition 3.12. Let A be totally disconnected nilperfectoid. A Cartier divisor D ⊆YA is called of
degree 1 if its pullback D ×YA YA is a degree 1 Cartier divisor in YA.
Remark 3.13. Note that any degree 1 Cartier divisor D ⊆YA is affine. Indeed, any degree 1 Cartier
divisor in YA is fully contained in YA,[r,s] for some [r, s] ⊆(0, ∞) and hence D is already a Cartier
divisor in YA,[r,s] = GSpec B†
A,[r,s].
△
Example 3.14. The map φ−1 ◦ι : GSpec A →YA is a degree 1 Cartier divisor. This is because the
image of this composition is supported over {1/p} ⊆(0, ∞) under the radius map and YA is isomorphic
to YA over (0, 1) by Proposition 3.10.
△

4. Analytic syntomification over Qp
We now move on to defining the analytic syntomification XSyn of a Gelfand stack X and studying its
geometry. To set the stage, recall how one proceeds for the “algebraic” syntomification in the sense of
Bhatt–Lurie and Drinfeld, see [Bha22] or [Dri22]. There, the main part of the work lies in constructing
a filtered refinement of the prismatisation of Zp called the (Nygaard-)filtered prismatisation or the
Nygaardification of Zp; from here, it is not too hard to define the Nygaardification of any p-adic
formal scheme X. Finally, to obtain the syntomification, one then identifies two disjoint copies of the
prismatisation inside the Nygaardification and glues them together.
In our analytic situation, the picture will be entirely similar. However, to get to the filtered
prismatisation, we will take a slightly different approach than [Bha22] and [Dri22] and instead use a
convenient reformulation of the definition of the filtered prismatisation due to Gardner–Madapusi, see
[GM25, §6]. Namely, in loc. cit., they show that one can alternatively obtain the Nygaardification of
Zp directly from the prismatisation of Zp by taking a suitable pullback, and this will be the definition
that we will transport to the analytic setting.
Even more so than the analytic prismatisation, the analytic Nygaardification XN of a Gelfand stack
X will come with a plethora of extra structures, some of which we list here:
(i) There is a map π : XN →X∆, via which XN acquires a radius map defined as the composite
κ : XN →X∆→(0, ∞).
(ii) There is a map t : XN →D/T and a map u : XN →(D/T)dR.
(iii) There are isomorphisms X∆∼= XN
|t|=1 and X∆∼= XN
|u|=1 and we denote the corresponding closed
embeddings X∆→XN by jdR and jHT, respectively. Moreover, the composition π ◦jdR is the
identity while π ◦jHT is the Frobenius on X∆.
(iv) There is an isomorphism XN
|ut|̸=0 ∼= (X∆\XdR)×[0, 1], where we view XdR as a closed substack
of X∆via idR.
(v) There is an isomorphism XdR,+ ∼= XN
|u|=0 and we denote the corresponding closed embedding
XdR,+ →XN by idR,+.
The situation is summarised by the schematic pictures in Fig. 3 and Fig. 4, the first of which studies
XN via the maps (t, u) : XN →D/T × (D/T)dR while the second studies XN via the radius map
κ : XN →(0, ∞).
At the end of the section, we will prove two somewhat technical results that will be invaluable
several times later on: First, we prove that the assignment sending a derived Berkovich space X to its
Nygaardification XN preserves Berkovich ´etale maps and that XN →Y N is a !-cover if X →Y is a
Berkovich ´etale !-cover of derived Berkovich spaces. This will be very useful later on when we study
Nygaardifications of Berkovich smooth derived Berkovich spaces as it basically allows us to reduce to
the case where X = T
n is the overconvergent n-dimensional torus. Second, we show that the stack
QN
p,[r,s] is nicely coverable in the sense of Definition 2.11 whenever p1/2 < r ≤s < p3/2; using the
results of the next section, this will allow us to conclude that the restriction of the Nygaardification of
T
n to any [r, s] ⊆(p1/2, p3/2) is nicely coverable for all n ≥0. This will later enable us to apply the
spreading out results for perfect complexes from §2 to XN for any smooth derived Berkovich space X.

4.4. Compatibility with ´etale localisation. We now want to prove compatibility of the functor X 7→XN
with Berkovich ´etale localisation. For this, we first extend the definition of Berkovich ´etale maps
between derived Berkovich spaces from [ABLB+25, Def. 4.3.4.(3)] to maps between arbitrary Gelfand
stacks as follows.
Definition 4.19. Let X →Y be a map of Gelfand stacks. It is called Berkovich ´etale if X ×Y GSpec A
is a derived Berkovich space for any totally disconnected nilperfectoid A with a map GSpec A →Y
and the map
X ×Y GSpec A →GSpec A
is a Berkovich ´etale map of derived Berkovich spaces in the sense of [ABLB+25, Def. 4.3.4.(3)].


## Page 53


53
Similarly, we can define finite ´etale maps between Gelfand stacks as well as open embeddings
and rational localisations, and we note that all these notions will be stable under base change as an
immediate consequence of the definition. With this notation in place, the result we want to prove
reads as follows.
Theorem 4.20. Let f : X →Y be a Berkovich ´etale map of derived Berkovich spaces. Then the induced
map f N : XN →Y N is Berkovich ´etale as well. Moreover, if f is a !-cover, then the same is true for
the map f N.
Note that, according to the discussion of various loci inside XN above, the theorem above implies
the corresponding statement for all other stacks associated to p-adic cohomology theories we have
introduced so far, e.g. XdR,+ or X∆, by suitable base changes.
To prove Theorem 4.20, recall that a Berkovich ´etale map is defined as being finite ´etale locally in a
strict closed cover of the source and target by rational localisations. Thus, the theorem we want to
prove breaks up into two parts and we start by treating finite ´etale maps. Before we begin the proof,
let us mention that the key is that the finite ´etale site is invariant under tilting and under †-reduction,
see [ABLB+25, Prop. 3.2.11] for the latter, together with the fact that finite ´etale maps are †-formally
´etale.
Proposition 4.21. Let f : X →Y be a finite ´etale map of affine Gelfand stacks. Then the induced map
f N : XN →Y N is finite ´etale. In particular, f N is a !-cover.

A-valued point of Y N lying over an A-valued point (D ⊆YA, L →A, A →K) of QN

5. Nygaardifications of smooth derived Berkovich spaces
Our aim in this section is to find explicit local presentations of some Nygaardifications. While
the need for this might not be fully apparent at the moment, having such explicit presentations will
be very useful later when we want to show that QN
p is cohomologically smooth and that the functor
X 7→XN carries rigid smooth maps of derived Berkovich spaces to cohomologically smooth maps and
quasicompact locally finitely presented maps of derived Berkovich spaces to weakly cohomologically
proper maps.
Let us give a brief idea of what we would like to do. We immediately warn the reader that this will
ultimately not quite work and cause us to work with a slight variant of QN
p instead, but it still seems
valuable to explain why we introduce this variant and why it is easier to handle than QN
p itself. Trying
to find an explicit local presentation of QN
p , we first think about Q∆

6. Six functors for the syntomification
Using the explicit presentations of Nygaardifications from the previous section, we are now in a
position to prove that the functor X 7→XSyn interacts nicely with the six functor formalism on Gelfand
stacks. More explicitly, we want to prove the following properties:
(1) The map QSyn
p
→GSpec Qp is cohomologically smooth with dualising sheaf O{1}[3].
(2) If f : X →Y is a rigid smooth map of derived Berkovich spaces over Qp which is pure of
relative dimension d, then the induced map fSyn : XSyn →Y Syn is cohomologically smooth
with dualising sheaf O{d}[2d].
(3) If f : X →Y is a map of derived Berkovich spaces over Qp which is locally of finite presentation
and quasicompact, then f Syn is weakly cohomologically proper in the sense of [RC24, Def.
3.1.19].
Finally, we will show that analytic syntomic cohomology affords a strong theory of first Chern classes
in the sense of [Zav26, Def. 5.2.8]. In particular, for any derived Berkovich space X, there is a natural
map
cSyn
1
: RΓ(XBerk-´et, Gm)[1] →RΓ(XSyn, O{1}[2]) ,
where XBerk-´et denotes the Berkovich ´etale site of X, and this induces a natural isomorphism between
the pushforward of the structure sheaf along (P1)Syn →QSyn
p
and the sheaf O ⊕O{−1}[−2].
In particular, this will let us deduce the following form of relative Poincar´e duality for analytic
syntomic cohomology: For any rigid smooth morphism f : X →Y of derived Berkovich spaces which
is quasicompact and any perfect analytic F-gauge E ∈Perf(XSyn) on X, the pushforward fSyn
∗

perfect on Y Syn and there is an isomorphism

p
, the
only form of Poincar´e duality we can obtain is an isomorphism

for any rigid smooth derived Berkovich space X over Qp which is pure of relative dimension d and
any perfect analytic F-gauge E ∈Perf(XSyn) on X; here, RΓc denotes the !-pushforward to the point.
Note that one can somewhat “see” the failure of QSyn
p
to be weakly cohomologically proper from the
geometry: Namely, this should intuitively be explained by the fact that QN
p is “open at ∞” with respect
to the radius map κ : QN
p →(0, ∞).
Let us quickly say something about the strategy of the proofs: As we can fully describe XN away
from p ∈(0, ∞) in terms of XDiv1 and the Hyodo–Kato stack XHK and both cohomological smoothness
as well as weak cohomological properness may be checked locally on the source (in an open or finite
closed cover, respectively), the main problem is to get a handle on XN

previous section provides explicit presentations in the cases X = Gm and X = GSpec Qp.

of the stack

Having Proposition 6.10, we can now easily deduce the following general cohomological smoothness
result for maps between syntomifications:
Theorem 6.14. Let f : X →Y be a rigid smooth map of derived Berkovich spaces. Then the induced
map f Syn : XSyn →Y Syn is cohomologically smooth. If f is pure of relative dimension d, then the
dualising sheaf is given by O{d}[2d].
Proof. As cohomological smoothness is local on the target, it suffices to check the claim for f N : XN →
Y N. Then recall from Proposition 4.21 and Proposition 4.22 that the functor X 7→XN preserves finite
´etale maps and open localisations. As finite ´etale maps and open embeddings are cohomologically
´etale and cohomological smoothness may be checked locally on the target and smooth-locally on the
source, we may reduce to the case X = Ad
Y . By commutation of X 7→XN with limits and stability
of cohomological smoothness under base change and composition, we may then further reduce to
Y = GSpec Qp and X = A1. Finally, we perform a further open localisation on the source by covering
A1 by Gm and 1 + Gm, and this allows us to reduce to X = Gm.
However, now note that GN
m,(p1/2,p3/2) →QN
p,(p1/2,p3/2) is cohomologically smooth by Proposition 6.10.
Moreover, we have
GN
m,|ut|̸=0 ∼= (G∆
m \ GdR
m ) × [0, 1]
by Proposition 4.9 and similarly for QN
p,|ut|̸=0, whence the map GN

p,|ut|̸=0 is cohomologically
smooth by Corollary 3.32. As QN
p,(p1/2,p3/2) and QN
p,|ut|̸=0 form an open cover of QN
p , this proves the
claim by locality of cohomological smoothness on the target.
Overall, we conclude that f Syn : XSyn →Y Syn is cohomologically smooth and it remains to compute
the dualising sheaf. Going through the argument above, we already see that it is a line bundle
concentrated in cohomological degree −2d. As at the end of the proof of Theorem 6.1, we can use
Theorem 9.16 below to conclude that it suffices to identify the pullback of this line bundle to X∆
(0,p)
together with the automorphism induced by Frobenius. However, using smooth base change, see
[HM24, Lem. 4.5.13], this reduces to the computation of the dualising sheaf of fDiv1 : XDiv1 →Y Div1,
and we already know that this is O{d}[2d] by Theorem 3.31. This finishes the proof.
□
Corollary 6.15. Let X be a rigid smooth derived Berkovich space over Qp which is pure of dimension d
and let f : XSyn →GSpec Qp be the structure map. For any perfect analytic F-gauge E ∈D(XSyn),

RΓSyn(X, Qp(i)) ∼= RΓSyn,c(X, Qp(2d + 1 −i))∨[−2d −3]
for any i ∈Z.

Remark 6.16. Let us note that any smooth map between partially proper rigid spaces is rigid smooth
and hence satisfies the assumption of Theorem 6.14.
△


## Page 89


89
Finally, we would also like to establish a properness statement for maps between syntomifications.
For this, we first make the following definition:
Definition 6.17. Let X →Y be a map between derived Berkovich spaces. It is called locally of finite
presentation if, after passing to a strict closed cover of the source and target by rational localisations,
it is of the form GSpec B →GSpec A for B a finitely presented animated A-algebra.
Remark 6.18. We emphasise that the finite presentation assumption above is in the derived sense.
This means that a morphism of classical rings A →B is finitely presented in this sense if and only if it
factors as A →A[x1, . . . , xd] →B, where A[x1, . . . , xd] →B is the quotient by a regular sequence.
△
Using this terminology, the properness statement we prove can be formulated as follows. We note
that it is likely that the finite presentation assumption below can be weakened, but we have chosen to
stick to this version as it is more than enough for our purposes.
Theorem 6.19. Let f : X →Y be a map between derived Berkovich spaces which is quasicompact and
locally of finite presentation. Then the induced map f Syn : XSyn →Y Syn is weakly cohomologically
proper.
Before we begin the proof, let us establish the following easier variant, to which we will later reduce
by a d´evissage:
Proposition 6.20. Let f : X →Y be a Berkovich smooth map between derived Berkovich spaces which
is quasicompact. Then the induced map f Syn : XSyn →Y Syn is weakly cohomologically proper.
Proof. We first check that f Syn is prim. This may be done locally on the target and hence reduces
to checking primness of f N : XN →Y N. Now recall from Proposition 4.21 and Proposition 4.22 that
X 7→XN preserves finite ´etale maps and rational localisations and note that any finite ´etale map or
rational localisation is cohomologically proper; in particular, the functor (−)N sends Berkovich ´etale
maps to cohomologically proper maps. As the map f : X →Y is quasicompact and primness is local
on the target as well as local for finite closed covers of the source, we may assume that f factors as
X →Ad
Y →Y with the first map being Berkovich ´etale, hence inducing a cohomologically proper map
Nygaardifications. By quasicompactness of f, the map X →Ad

which we may assume to be D

and Y = GSpec Qp. Finally, we may embed D into T as the closed subspace 1 + D(1/p) and hence we
may assume X = T.

7. p-adic Hodge theory for rigid-analytic varieties revisited
Having developed plenty of theory around the syntomification in the previous sections, we now want
to show how to put it to use. As our first goal, we want to show how one can recover some results from
Scholze’s paper [Sch13a] using the syntomification. To this end, we are going to analyse the equivalence
Vect(XdR,+) ∼= Vect(XHT,†,+)
(7.0.1)
from Proposition 5.18 and relate it to the functor
{filtered vector bundles with connection on X} →{B+
dR-local systems on Xpro´et}
(7.0.2)
from [Sch13a, §7].
Namely, from (7.0.1), we obtain a natural functor
Vect(XdR,+) ∼= Vect(XHT,†,+) →Vect(XHT,†)
→Vect((“
X ⊆YX♦)∧) ∼= {B+
dR-local systems on Xpro´et}
via pullback and we are going to identify this functor with (7.0.2). Indeed, using the results of §5.3, we
will be able to make the above functor explicit in terms of linear algebra when X admits a Berkovich
´etale map X →T
n and, in this case, our claim reduces to a fairly simple computation. Then the key is
to show that these local identifications glue, i.e. that they are independent of the chosen toric chart
X →T
n.
Finally, we will also be able to recover the classical de Rham comparison theorem
RΓpro´et(XCp, Qp) ⊗Qp BdR ∼= RΓdR(X) ⊗Qp BdR
for smooth proper rigid spaces X over Qp using the syntomification. In fact, we will even be able to
recover the relative version for smooth proper morphisms X →Y from [Sch13a, Thm. 8.8] and, as
in loc. cit., using the results of §9, we will also be able to allow for coefficients in any de Rham local
system on Xpro´et.
7.1. Scholze’s functor via the syntomification. To begin, recall the structural de Rham sheaf OBdR
on the pro´etale site Xpro´et of any smooth partially proper rigid space X over Qp defined in [Sch13a,
§6], see also [Sch16]. This is a BdR-algebra equipped with a descending filtration Fil• OBdR and a

8. The comparison with filtered Hyodo–Kato cohomology
Recall that the main utility of syntomic cohomology classically is that it relates to both “de Rham”
and “´etale data” via comparison theorems such as the ones proved in [CN20] or [CN25]. In this spirit,
we want to use the present section to show how perfect complexes on XSyn and their cohomology
can be understood through a “de Rham lens” for Berkovich smooth derived Berkovich spaces X; in
particular, this includes smooth partially proper rigid spaces over Qp and thus also relates to more
classical settings of p-adic analytic geometry.
More specifically, we are going to show that perfect complexes on the syntomification XSyn of a
Berkovich smooth derived Berkovich space and their cohomology can be completely understood via
perfect complexes on the Hyodo–Kato stack XHK and on the filtered de Rham stack XdR,+ of X.
Namely, we are going to construct a commutative diagram
XdR

and show that it induces an equivalence of categories
Perf(XSyn) ∼= Perf(XHK) ×Perf(XdR) Perf(XdR,+)
(8.0.2)
via pullback. We note that this result should be compared to the so-called Beilinson fibre square for
syntomic cohomology of p-adic formal schemes in its stacky formulation from [Hau25, §3], where the
occurrence of the Hyodo–Kato stack above is replaced by the algebraic syntomification of the special
fibre.
In particular, the equivalence (8.0.2) includes the statement that the syntomic cohomology of X
with coefficients in a perfect analytic F-gauge may be computed as a pullback of the Hyodo–Kato
cohomology and the Hodge-filtered de Rham cohomology over the full de Rham cohomology of X,
each with suitable coefficients obtained from the perfect analytic F-gauge we started with through
various realisation functors, which we shall introduce. Taking coefficients in a Breuil–Kisin twist and
comparing with [CN20, Thm. 1.1], this in particular implies that our definition of RΓSyn(X, Qp(i)) for
smooth partially proper qcqs rigid spaces X recovers the one of Colmez–Nizio l.
Finally, applying the above discussion to the case X = GSpec Qp, we will be able to identify vector
bundles on QSyn
p
with GalQp-equivariant vector bundles on FFCp which are de Rham in the sense of
[FF18, Def. 15.12]. Moreover, in the case where the de Rham bundle arises from a Qp-representation
V of GalQp by extending scalars, we will see that

where the right-hand side was defined by Bloch–Kato in [BK90] as the subspace of H1(GalQp, V )
spanned by classes corresponding to extensions of Qp by V which are de Rham.
One should compare this to similar of result of Bhatt–Lurie about the algebraic syntomification of Zp:
In [Bha22, §§6.6, 6.7], they prove that Zp-lattices in crystalline GalQp-representations are equivalent to
the category of so-called reflexive coherent sheaves on the algebraic syntomification of Zp and that
this equivalence identifies H1 on the syntomification with the Bloch–Kato–Selmer group H1
f after
rationalisation, see [Bha22, Prop. 6.7.3].

Using Proposition 8.2, the diagram (8.0.1) now induces various realisation functors for perfect
analytic F-gauges. Firstly, pullback along the map iHK induces a realisation functor
THK : Perf(XSyn) →Perf(XHK)


## Page 104

from perfect analytic F-gauges to perfect complexes on XHK, which should be thought of as coefficients
for Hyodo–Kato cohomology of X following [ABLB+25, Rem. 6.1.3]. Secondly, via pullback along the
filtered de Rham map idR,+, we obtain a realisation functor
TdR,+ : D(XSyn) →D(XdR,+)
from analytic F-gauges to quasicoherent sheaves on XdR,+, i.e. a category of filtered analytic D-modules
on X, and, lastly, postcomposing TdR,+ with pullback along the canonical map XdR →XdR,+ yields a
realisation functor
TdR : D(XSyn) →D(XdR) .
Note that pullback along the map XdR →XHK,mock from (8.1.2) also induces a functor Perf(XHK) →
Perf(XdR) whose precomposition with THK identifies with TdR by commutativity of the diagram (8.0.1).
Using this terminology, we can formulate the main result of this section as follows:
Theorem 8.3. Let X be a Berkovich smooth derived Berkovich space over Qp. Then there is an
equivalence of categories
Perf(XSyn) ∼= Perf(XHK) ×Perf(XdR) Perf(XdR,+)
induced by the realisation functors THK and TdR,+. In particular, for any E ∈Perf(XSyn), there is a
pullback diagram
RΓ(XSyn, E)

RΓ(XdR,+, TdR,+(E))
RΓ(XdR, TdR(E)) .
Using the expected relation between the stack XHK and Hyodo–Kato cohomology of X, comparing
the result above with [CN20, Thm. 1.1] in particular shows that our definition of syntomic cohomology
recovers the more classical definition of syntomic cohomology of Colmez–Nizio l for smooth partially
proper qcqs rigid spaces over Qp, see e.g. [CN20, §4.4]. Indeed, applying the above result to E = O{i}
and using Proposition 2.40, we obtain:
Corollary 8.4. Let X be a smooth partially proper qcqs rigid space over Qp. For any i ∈Z, there is a

9. The comparison with pro´etale cohomology
We now play the game from the previous section in the other direction: Instead of approaching the
syntomification of Berkovich smooth derived Berkovich spaces from the de Rham side, we will now
exploit the relation between XSyn and “´etale data” of X. In fact, this will work using almost the same
arguments as in the previous section, but “in the other direction”, i.e. we have to swap the roles of u
and t and reverse the direction on the κ-axis.
Namely, we are going to prove that, for a Berkovich smooth derived Berkovich space X, perfect
complexes on XSyn and their cohomology can be understood via perfect complexes on XDiv1 and on
the stack XHT,†,+ that we have introduced previously. This will again take the form of a commutative
diagram
XHT,†
XHT,†,+
XDiv1,mock
XSyn ,
iHT,†,+
iDiv1
(9.0.1)
where XDiv1,mock should again be thought of as XDiv1 for all intents and purposes, for which we are
going to show that it induces an equivalence of categories
Perf(XSyn) ∼= Perf(XDiv1) ×Perf(XHT,†) Perf(XHT,†,+) .
(9.0.2)
Let us point out that, given the fact that the corresponding result (8.0.2) in the “de Rham case”
has an analogue for p-adic formal schemes in terms of the algebraic syntomification of Drinfeld and
Bhatt–Lurie, it seems to be an interesting question to investigate to which extent the naive analogue
of (9.0.2) holds for the algebraic syntomification of smooth p-adic formal schemes.
As the equivalence (9.0.2) again entails a fibre product formula expressing syntomic cohomology of
X with coefficients in a perfect analytic F-gauge in terms of cohomology on XDiv1, XHT,†,+ and XHT,†,
we will be able to recover the comparison between syntomic cohomology and pro´etale cohomology
of a smooth partially proper rigid space X in a suitable range from [CN20, Thm. 1.1]. Indeed, as
cohomology on XDiv1 agrees with pro´etale cohomology by [ALBRCS], this comes down to controlling
the difference between cohomology on XHT,†,+ and XHT,†, which is precisely responsible for the
necessary truncation in the comparison theorem.
Finally, we will make use of the equivalence (9.0.2) above to explicitly describe vector bundles on
the syntomification of any smooth partially proper rigid space X over Qp. Namely, we are going to
prove that there is an equivalence
Vect(XSyn) ∼= VectdR(FFX♦)
between vector bundles on XSyn and vector bundles on FFX♦which are de Rham in a certain sense
to be defined below, see Definition 9.14. For this, we are going to show that the pullback functor
Vect(XHT,†,+) →Vect(XHT,†) is fully faithful. To determine its essential image and make the de
Rham condition appear, we are going to use the main result of §7: Namely, the functor
Vect(XdR,+) ∼= Vect(XHT,†,+) →Vect(XHT,†)
→Vect((“
X ⊆YX♦)∧) ∼= {B+

from [Sch13a, §7] and hence the functor Vect(XHT,†,+) →Vect(XHT,†) automatically factors through
a certain full subcategory of “generically flat” vector bundles on XHT,† to be defined below, see
Definition 9.21. The hardest part will then be to show that this factorisation is already essentially
surjective and this will involve an explicit computation using differential operators, which we carry out
in Lemma 9.23.

Using Proposition 9.2, the diagram (9.0.1) induces various realisation functors for perfect analytic
F-gauges. Firstly, pullback along the map iDiv1 induces a realisation functor
TDiv1 : Perf(XSyn) →Perf(XDiv1)
from perfect analytic F-gauges to perfect complexes on XDiv1, which should be thought of as coefficients
for pro´etale cohomology of X following [ALBRCS]. Secondly, via pullback along the map iHT,†,+ from
above, we obtain a realisation functor
THT,†,+ : D(XSyn) →D(XHT,†,+)
from analytic F-gauges to quasicoherent sheaves on XHT,†,+ and, lastly, postcomposing THT,†,+ with
pullback along the canonical map XHT,† →XHT,†,+ yields a realisation functor
THT,† : D(XSyn) →D(XHT,†) .
Note that pullback along the map XHT,† →XDiv1,mock from (9.1.1) induces a functor Perf(XDiv1) →
Perf(XHT,†) whose precomposition with THK identifies with THT,† by commutativity of the diagram
(9.0.1). This enables us to formulate the “´etale” analogue of Theorem 8.3:
Theorem 9.3. Let X be a Berkovich smooth derived Berkovich space over Qp. Then there is an
equivalence of categories
Perf(XSyn) ∼= Perf(XDiv1) ×Perf(XHT,†) Perf(XHT,†,+)

rigid spaces X over Qp by [ALBRCS], the result above lets us recover the classical comparison theorem

1.1.(4)].
Theorem 9.4. Let X be a smooth partially proper rigid space over Qp. If E ∈Vect(XSyn) is a vector
bundle analytic F-gauge with Hodge–Tate weights all at most −i for some i ≥0, then the natural
morphism
RΓ(XSyn, E) →RΓ(XDiv1, TDiv1(E))
is an isomorphism on τ ≤i and induces an injection on Hi+1. In particular, for E = O{i}, we obtain
τ ≤iRΓSyn(X, Qp(i)) ∼= τ ≤iRΓpro´et(X, Qp(i)) .
Proof. By Theorem 9.3, our task is to prove that the cofibre of the morphism
RΓ(XHT,†,+, THT,†,+(E)) →RΓ(XHT,†, THT,†(E))
is concentrated in degrees at least i+1. By compatibility of the functors X 7→XHT,†,+ and X 7→XHT,†
with open localisation, which one deduces from Proposition 4.22, we are reduced to the case where
X admits a Berkovich ´etale map X →T
n. However, in this case, Corollary 5.17 gives precise double
complexes computing the cohomologies in question. Indeed, inspecting these double complexes, we see
that our claim follows once we show that the ascending filtration Fil• V associated to the restriction of
E to XHT,†,+ via Corollary 5.15 stabilises from Fil−i V onwards, i.e.
Fil−i V = Fil−i+1 V = . . . .
To see this, recall from Corollary 5.15 that the (descendingly) filtered vector bundle with connection
(Fil• W, ∇) on X corresponding to the restriction of E to XdR,+ is obtained by Tate twisting and then
descending the associated graded pieces of Fil• V to X, i.e. grk V descends to Filk W up to a Tate
twist, and hence we equivalently have to show that
· · · = Fil−i+2 W = Fil−i+1 W = 0 .
However, our assumption on the Hodge–Tate weights precisely means that grk W = 0 for k ≥−i + 1
and now we are done by noting that the decreasing filtration Fil• W is separated, which follows e.g.
from Corollary 5.15.
□
Remark 9.5. Note that Theorem 9.3 in some sense improves upon the “classical” comparison from
Theorem 9.4 by also describing the failure of the map RΓ(XSyn, O{i}) →RΓpro´et(X, Qp(i)) to be an
isomorphism in higher degrees: Namely, this is exactly measured by the failure of RΓ(XHT,†,+, O{i}) →
RΓ(XHT,†, O{i}) to be an isomorphism.
△
Of course, combining Theorem 9.4 with Theorem 8.3, we immediately obtain the following extension
of Colmez–Nizio l’s “basic comparison theorem” from [CN20, Thm. 1.3] to vector bundle F-gauge
coefficients:

yields the following result, which was already sketched in [ABLB+25, Rem. 7.5.7] and, as explained in
loc. cit., in particular also implies Shimizu’s theorem about de Rham local systems from [Shi22, Thm.
1.1]:
Corollary 9.20. Let X be a smooth partially proper rigid space over Qp. Then there is an equivalence
of categories

Let us now come back to proving Theorem 9.16. For this, recall from Proposition 3.19 that there is
a natural map YX♦→X∆for any Gelfand stack X and, similarly, one proves that there is a natural

Qp, furthermore recall from [ALBRCS] that pullback along this map induces an equivalence
Vect(XDiv1) ∼= Vect(FFX♦) .
Thus, in view of Theorem 9.3, the main part of the proof of Theorem 9.16 is to understand the pullback
functor Vect(XHT,†,+) →Vect(XHT,†).
More precisely, we see that our task is to prove that this functor is fully faithful and that its essential
image is exactly cut out by the de Rham condition in Theorem 9.3. More to the point, recall from

Definition 9.21. Let X be a smooth partially proper rigid space over Qp. A vector bundle on XHT,† is
called generically flat if its associated B+
dR-local system on Xpro´et is generically flat.
With this terminology in place, the above discussion shows that the proof of Theorem 9.16 reduces
to the following statement:
Proposition 9.22. Let X be a smooth partially proper rigid space over Qp. Then the pullback functor
Vect(XHT,†,+) →Vect(XHT,†)
is fully faithful and its essential image is given by the full subcategory of vector bundles on XHT,† which
are generically flat.
From Theorem 7.1, we can already conclude that the pullback functor Vect(XHT,†,+) →Vect(XHT,†)
factors through the full subcategory of vector bundles on XHT,† which are generically flat.

10. Geometric syntomic cohomology
Finally, we want to comment on how to apply the methods we have developed to the geometric
case, i.e. to the cohomology of rigid spaces over Cp. For this, first recall the situation for the de Rham
stack: By [ABLB+25, Ex. 4.7.6.(3)], we have CdR
p
∼= GSpec Qp and hence just taking the de Rham
stack cannot compute the usual de Rham cohomology of smooth partially proper rigid spaces over Cp.
Indeed, the problem is that the stack XdR always computes “absolute de Rham cohomology”, i.e. de
Rham cohomology with respect to the base Qp, while the usual de Rham cohomology of rigid spaces
over Cp is rather relative to Cp. In other words, if X is a Gelfand stack over Cp, we should define an
appropriate version XdR/Cp of XdR “relative to Cp”. Meditating on the situation, one is eventually led
to defining XdR/Cp via the pullback square
XdR/Cp
GSpec Cp
XdR
CdR
p
,
where GSpec Cp →CdR
p
is the canonical map, see [ABLB+25, Def. 4.5.11.(2)]. One then proves as
before that the cohomology of this stack calculates de Rham cohomology if X is a smooth partially
proper rigid space over Cp, see [ABLB+25, Prop. 5.2.1].
Thus, the key issue in defining syntomic cohomology in the geometric situation, i.e. for Berkovich
smooth derived Berkovich spaces over Cp, is to identify the “correct” base change of XSyn or XN.
Below, we will explain that there is a canonical map
Fil YCp := ({ut = φ−1(ξ)} ⊆YCp × D+ × D−) / T →CN
p ,
where ξ = p −[p♭], and that base changing XN along this map yields an appropriate definition for the
filtered prismatisation XN/Cp of X relative to Cp. We will justify this claim by showing that basically
everything we have done in the “arithmetic case”, i.e. for smooth partially proper rigid space over Qp,
has an analogue in the geometric case with the definition of XN/Cp proposed above.
In particular, this includes the basic description of the geometry of the filtered prismatisation that
we have discussed in §4.2 and the local presentations of the filtered prismatisation obtained in §5. This
will enable us to transfer the results from §§8, 9 and their proofs almost verbatim to the geometric
setting. In particular, we recover versions of the comparison results between syntomic cohomology
over Cp and filtered Hyodo–Kato cohomology and between syntomic cohomology over Cp and pro´etale
cohomology from [CN25, Thm. 1.3]. However, we warn the reader that, in the geometric case, syntomic
cohomology as we define it will not agree with the more classical definition as it appears e.g. in the
work of Colmez–Nizio l, see [CN25], but will rather recover a slight variant that has been introduced by
Bosco in [Bos23b], where it is called syntomic Fargues–Fontaine cohomology, see also Remark 10.17.
On the way, we will obtain a stacky interpretation of the B+
dR-cohomology for smooth partially
proper rigid spaces over Cp as e.g. defined in [CN25, §3], [Guo24] or [BMS18, §13] through a suitable
base change of the analytic de Rham stack, and this will enable us to give a simple proof for the
comparison between B+
dR-cohomology and de Rham cohomology. Moreover, similarly to what happened
in §7, we will be able to recover the geometric de Rham comparison theorem from [BMS18, Thm. 13.1],
see Theorem 10.12.
10.1. Stacks attached to p-adic cohomology theories relative to Cp. We start by recalling some
definitions of stacks computing p-adic cohomology theories relative to Cp. As already discussed above,
the main question throughout is along which map one should base change. To this end, recall from
Proposition 3.19 that there is a canonical map YX♦→X∆for any Gelfand stack X and that, similarly,
we have canonical maps FFX♦→XDiv1 and FFX♦→XHK.

with a graded perfect complex M • on X, the Hodge–Tate weights of E are defined to be those
integers i ∈Z such that M i is nonzero. The Hodge–Tate weights of a perfect analytic F-gauge
E ∈Perf(XSyn/Cp) on X relative to Cp are defined to be the Hodge–Tate weights of its pullback to
the stack XN/Cp.

Proof. As in Theorem 5.14, Corollary 5.15 and Corollary 5.17.

Using Proposition 10.20 and that cohomology on XDiv1/Cp computes pro´etale cohomology of X
by [ALBRCS] if X is a smooth partially proper rigid space over Cp, we deduce the following explicit
comparison theorem from Theorem 10.19:
Theorem 10.21. Let X be a smooth partially proper rigid space over Cp. If E ∈Vect(XSyn/Cp) is a
vector bundle analytic F-gauge on X relative to Cp with Hodge–Tate weights all at most −i for some
i ≥0, then the natural morphism
RΓ(XSyn/Cp, E) →RΓ(XDiv1/Cp, TDiv1(E))
is an isomorphism on τ ≤i and induces an injection on Hi+1. In particular, for E = O{i}, we obtain
τ ≤iRΓSyn(X/Cp, Qp(i)) ∼= τ ≤iRΓpro´et(X, Qp(i)) .
Proof. As in Theorem 9.4.
□
Finally, we can again combine the results of Theorem 10.14 and Theorem 10.21 to obtain a version
of Colmez–Nizio l’s “basic comparison theorem” in the geometric case, see [CN25, Thm. 1.1], with vector
bundle F-gauge coefficients. However, as already pointed out above, our version differs from the one
in loc. cit. due to the appearance of the period ring Blog in place of B+
st and should thus rather be
compared to the result of Bosco from [Bos23b, Thm. 7.2].
Corollary 10.22. Let X be a smooth partially proper rigid space over Cp. If E ∈Vect(XSyn/Cp) is a
vector bundle analytic F-gauge on X relative to Cp with Hodge–Tate weights all at most −i for some
i ≥0, then there is a natural map
fib(RΓ(XHK/Cp, THK(E)) →RΓ(XdR/B+,†
dR , TdR(E))/RΓ(XdR,+/B+,†
dR ,TdR,+(E)))
→RΓ(XDiv1, TDiv1(E))
which induces an isomorphism on τ ≤i and an injection on Hi+1. In particular, if X is qcqs and
E = O{i}, we obtain

Proof. The first part follows by combining Theorem 10.14 and Theorem 10.21 while the second part
then follows using Corollary 10.16.
